### P-37

2026-10-13 18:16Z · rewritten · from `explorations/perf-probes/prelude/REPORT.md`, "5. The answer" · commit `bc8902f47` · by consolidation chunk P2: retitled to state the position; his words (2026-09-20, 2026-09-21) condensed to "a bootstrap to remove", their text in this history; the stopped prelude-name rungs are history
pointer words refreshed in: F-11

- P-37 **The library route.** "we need to unify this. into single library"; "the compiler second library is just a bootstrap and we need to get rid of it." One library, the interpreter's (`FortressLibrary` + `FortressBuiltin`), becomes the library the compiler checks; no declaration goes into the compiler's prelude (`CompilerLibrary`, `CompilerBuiltin`, `CompilerAlgebra`); the prelude-name rungs of the ladder stopped, code-generator rungs continue; the three prelude files are deleted at the switch-over and their tests kept. How the checker is brought to accept the library is a plan put to him step by step, not a decision to re-ask.

### P-38

2026-10-13 18:16Z · rewritten · from `explorations/reviews/wrap-dependent-code.md`, "10. The options as they would be put to Pavol" · commit `bc8902f47` · by consolidation chunk P2: retitled to state the position; the guard's lines (CompilerBuiltin.fss:601, :686) and the count of 10 compiled test files left to the report and this history

- P-38 **The compiler prelude's leftovers go at the switch-over.** The compiler-prelude block for `Maybe` stands as landed, its tests kept, its lines gone then; the guard `if other = -1 then 0` in the two compiled `REM` bodies (`ProjectFortress/LibraryBuiltin/CompilerBuiltin.fss:601`, `:686`) stays and goes with the prelude; the prelude's reversed spellings of the wrapping and saturating operators (row 348) are respelled then, in 10 compiled test files; `ZZ64`'s compiled shift declaration (row 381) closes when the compiled path reads this library; the specification's `shift` (row 383) reaches the compiled path then, `compiler_tests/XXXShiftDeclRungI.test` promoted and `shift` a reserved name on the compiled path from then on, the switch-over batch measuring which compiler tests bind it.

### P-39

2026-10-13 18:16Z · rewritten · from `explorations/perf-probes/prelude/switch-over-distance.md`, "The answers" · commit `bc8902f47` · by consolidation chunk P2: the distance's value is F-53's

- P-39 **The checker count is measured, never red.** The checker-count stage stays in every gate, reported and never red on its own; the full distance measurement (all apis and components) is a report-only stage of the phase-3 gates, whose job is driving it down.

### P-40

2026-10-13 18:16Z · rewritten · from `explorations/reviews/nat-checking-plan.md`, "(c) The minimal design" · commit `bc8902f47` · by consolidation chunk P2: retitled to state the position; "row 400 closed" corrected, since the ledger keeps row 400 open for its compiled half; "with row 41" dropped, since it named a re-approval row (the linker lookup, PLAN.md's item 12 of Pavol's answers), not ledger row 41; why sizes are NN32 and ranges ZZ32, and his words, left to this history

- P-40 **Sizes.** The checker checks `nat` and `int` parameters (built). A size left unknown after inference is an error only when it reaches a type, a name or a value, and nothing otherwise ("the middle is a clear winner"); how the checker represents an unknown size was not his to decide. Deleting the dead sizes (E3, 25 api declarations) is shown to him as a diff with the switch-over's design, with row 41. Every static argument counts in the exclusion rule except operator arguments ("sizes count"); booleans follow once the checker supports boolean parameters (row 406). A size the call cannot fix is an error at that call, also inside overload sets: when the arm the rules would pick has a size the call cannot fix, the call is refused with the same "could not infer" error instead of the arm being dropped (row 400 closed). A `nat` parameter is an `NN32` value and an `int` parameter a `ZZ32` (`Specification/basic/trait-parameters.tex:82-90`); a larger one is refused; the array design refuses a JVM array length over 2^31-1 where it makes storage ("that's not even a question"). A size used as a value converts to `ZZ32` as a numeral does (row 485); sizes as `NN32` and ranges as `ZZ32` are consistent, since array lengths and indices are Java `int`, a size is never negative and ranges count through negatives. All built.

### P-41

2026-10-13 18:16Z · rewritten · from `explorations/reviews/size-runtime-design-brief.md`, "11. Recommendation" · commit `bc8902f47` · by consolidation chunk P2: retitled to state the position; his words left to this history; the built state is F-129's
pointer words refreshed in: F-39, F-129

- P-41 **The run-time size design is B.** A size at run time gets the same descriptor object a type argument has, filling the slot the team's code generator reserved ("of course, let's extend the runtime object with a new field for the size"); one descriptor per number from a table, no holder class, the team's instantiation pattern applied to numbers. Operators are not unified with the descriptor-bearing kinds ("I don't see a need"). Built.

### P-69

2026-10-13 19:09Z · rewritten · from `explorations/compile-ladder/rung-int-semantics-walk/REPORT.md`, "5. The `shift` declaration: the collision, and the renames the specification requires" · commit `bc8902f47` · by consolidation chunk P3: "the smart-shift rule" spelled out; which types declare the shift pair, and `ZZ64`'s api lines, left to the report; his quote condensed

- P-69 **`LSHIFT`/`RSHIFT` are bit operators; `shift` is exact on ℤ** (row 335). On the fixed widths `LSHIFT`/`RSHIFT` are bit operators with the smart-shift rule, since every peer language lets shifted-out bits fall silently ("if every other language does something else, that's the clearest red flag to stop and pause and reconsider"), and the specification's arithmetic `shift` is exact on ℤ. His reservation for the code generator: on the fixed widths the compiled code shifts the machine word, never through ℤ. Built on both paths; `shift` reaches the compiled path at the switch-over. A `ZZ32` is shifted by a count of any integral width (rows 380, 381): `ZZ32`'s shift pair is declared with any integral count, as `Integral[\I\]` promises and as `ZZ`, `NN64` and `ZZ64`'s implementation do, `ZZ64`'s api lines matching its implementation; built under walk, the compiled side at the switch-over.

The test captures the essence of the problem, not the shape the probe met it in, "do not blindly transpose the issue from the probe like think about it what's the core problem and capture that", written as a clean minimal version. Not that it should be repeating the whole process, that is a fucking waste." Whether the suite runs per edit or per landed batch is an engineering choice, and per batch is in line with the rule. Every measured and repaired defect gets a gated assertion, a deferred spec-settled one an `XXX` test. The test needs no commit of its own: the order is test written, seen failing through the harness, the fix, the test seen passing, and the test and the fix may land in one commit ("I don't recall asking for test and fix to be separated into different commits ... ### P-125

2026-10-13 19:09Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/process-engineering/suites-at-four-threads.md`, "The short answer" · commit `8c8ace211` · by consolidation chunk P3: the macro names and the default without a pin left to F-99, which gives P-125's number

- P-125 **The suites run Fortress in parallel.** Parallelism is native to Fortress: tuple elements, arguments and `for` iterations run at once on the work-stealing pool. Pinning the suites to one thread (`FORTRESS_THREADS=1` in `build.xml`'s `fastTrack` and `systemShard`) made a blind spot, and sparing a four-CPU machine is no reason for it. A test that fails or varies under parallelism is written wrong, something in it needing to be atomic, or it exposes a defect; either is found and fixed, never hidden by the pin. Both suites pass at four threads with the gate's counts, so the pin in `build.xml` (`fastTrack` and `systemShard`) is `FORTRESS_THREADS=4`, not 1: every run parallel and the same on any machine, independent of the calling shell (without a pin the default is half the CPUs, 2 on this machine). Built (F-99, "The gate's thread count is pinned in the build file, not in the calling shell").

### P-104

2026-10-13 19:58Z · removed · by coordinator rewrite, step 12: left for the skills, its rule at its home: the coordinator skill, references/batch.md, "The workflow" and "The roles"; the fortress-repo skill, SKILL.md, "Rules for every task", and references/worktrees.md, "Seeding a worktree"

- P-104 **Nothing is built or run twice on the same code.** "We don't want to re be rebuilding stuff that doesn't need to be rebuilt." The design, agreed with him and built into the batch script: the old code, the batch's base, is built once per batch, by the coordinator, before the launch, in a worktree nobody compiles or runs in (the script's `args.baseBuild`); "We just mechanically take the latest build and share it between each rung from the same baseline": every rung worktree is seeded from that one build in seconds (`coordinator/tools/seed-worktree.sh`), never built clean; the worker builds only what its own edit changed; the skeptic builds nothing to check, "very smart skeptics that just like reuse everything that was built during the rank phase as much as possible and just do their independent checks": it reads test-first from the worker's transcript, runs its own small programs on the new code with the rung's build and on the old code from the batch's one base build, through `coordinator/tools/old-fortress.sh` with a private caches folder under the rung's worktree, so that no two agents compile into one cache. What the skeptic finds it fixes itself, test first, and a judge rules on a fix only where it is contested (P-118, "The judge's rulings."), his saving of the refusal chain; the fix is a new code state, built once and its suite run once, with no second skeptic after it and a repair round only on a judge's word. No stage re-runs a build, a suite or a measurement on a code state another stage has already run, and no agent reads again a log of output that has not changed: "I want to eliminate all waste of this type. Where we are re-executing stuff for no reason." How a worktree is seeded and what a worker clears after an edit or a failed build is engineering, measured before it is built into the batch script (`coordinator/build-cache-exploration.md`; the blinded engineers' variants in `coordinator/process-engineering/cache-sharing.md`; the redesign, `coordinator/process-engineering/batch-redesign.md`).

Ordinary workers carry no such procedure, and no ask is added before a probe: "I don't want you to become paranoid about running probes"; "I don't want the cargo cult of ... monitoring here. Watching the spend is the coordinator's, never the worker's: a brief does not ask a worker to measure, project or cap its own token spend, "it has its job. - P-112 **Which decisions taken inside the work reach Pavol, and how.** A decision an agent takes is consequential when it reverses a decision on record, bends it or reads it anew; changes a line of the model program; touches two or more of the specification, the library and the implementation; chooses among ways the record does not settle; or cannot be undone. Everything else an agent decided is listed for later review, a rung's small items in the ledger, and holds nothing up. A consequential decision reaches Pavol one of three ways: asked before the work, a fork known in advance probed and put in the ask form before the batch is briefed; landed, then reviewed, one that can be undone landing, named in one line at the landing, listed in PLAN under the batch's list with the default that landed and put to Pavol later, one per message; or a stop that holds the work, one that cannot be undone or would act against Pavol's word, put at once. The `coordinator` skill states it (`decisions.md`, "Decisions and how they reach the curator").

Workers are pinned to Opus by the alias, "to make that workflow future proof"; the expensive model is escalated only at decision points, "sparsely so that it doesn't have to gather all the context by running multiple tool calls". A fix the skeptic shows settled by the sentence on record it cites is not contested because it reaches a point to report or a path outside the section: it lands, and the point or the path is listed for him, as P-111, "Reversible stops do not hold a batch." lands any point to report. A judge's first ruling on a rung or on the merged tree runs on Opus; a second ruling on the same rung or tree runs on Fable ("Second ruling is for Fable."), named in `coordinator/climb-batch-workflow.js` (`judgeTier`), so Fable is spent only when a second ruling happens. The review's judge rules while the gate runs on, and the gate writes its outputs under `tmp/` until the commit stage. ### P-127

- P-127 **Check-ins and stops.** Every overnight or long background run, a batch or any other (a skill rewrite, a redesign worker), is watched by a series of check-ins spaced 45 minutes apart, armed when the run starts, so that the session stays in the prompt cache: hourly ones risk "that this session KV cache is evicted and then the wake-up call pays the full" price, and so would a run's completion arriving after a long silence. The one after a predicted process stop is a single extra. He sends messages at any time (a message during a turn is queued and kills nothing); he does not press stop while the session is working, including the seconds after a reply while the transcript backup runs; an accidental stop is said so, and the run is resumed, keeping every agent that had finished.

"I don't want you to be searching with a flashlight in a dark room. I want to give you a map of the territory." A FACTS entry is the fact, its source and its test in a few lines, pointing to the report or record that holds the detail, a rung's entry too, since rung entries written at full length, about 14 KB a landed batch, are most of FACTS' growth. First the skills. `fortress-repo` is rewritten whole by the outline and the seven principles, every part cold-read and fixed. He read and approved `SKILL.md`'s "How a program runs" and the three testing parts; the rest is not held for his reading before batch 11. The three approved parts go through the same pass, and a Fable reviewer compares them with the versions he approved, writing a synthesis of the two where it finds a regression, never a wholesale revert. `coordinator` and `cloud-container` follow later. Then the batch workflow is redesigned: an Opus synthesis of the six batch-10 designs and the measurements of where the batches' tokens went, built into the script with batch 11's manifest, and reviewed by Fable instead of by him. Then batch 11 runs, launched overnight on the standing go, its questions Q1 to Q4 at their recommendations, resumed after the weekly reset if the limit stops it; it is to show the better shape against the wasteful runs before it. The reason for the order: the recent climbing was token-inefficient because the batches re-ran their costly parts, and those causes are what started the skill extraction; a batch run before both are done repeats them.

The class cell is `kind (area)`, the area one of parser, walk, checker, codegen, runtime, library, prelude, specification, tests, tools; a specification citation gives the file and the section, a line number only against `Specification-1.0-frozen/`; a row is at most 1,200 characters, the open rows cut to it by the rewrite; the full earlier text of every changed row is in one history file, `explorations/fortress-gap-ledger-history.md` (D2 to D5 at the proposal's defaults). The rows are sorted into topic sections, the rewrite's topical groups becoming the sections, each row keeping its number; a new row goes into the section its writer names, and row 83 becomes an eight-cell row in its section (D6). `explorations/coordinator/tools/ledger.py` owns the ledger's format and is the only way to write it (add with the template checked, note, close, duplicate, check); `facts-extract.sh` stays the one reader of every record, its ledger queries calling `ledger.py`'s parser. ### P-134

2026-10-13 19:58Z · removed · by coordinator rewrite, step 12: left for the skills, its rule at its home: the coordinator skill, references/skill-rewrite.md, "The method"; references/delegating.md, "What a brief holds"; references/batch.md, "The checker count and the distance"; SKILL.md, "Talking to the curator"; the fortress-repo skill, SKILL.md, "When to explore" and "Rules for every task"; the writing principles and the writer's standing brief

- P-134 **The skills are written for a reader new to the repository, and checked against what the workers did.** The skills distil the best practices found by every worker in this repository, for a reader new to it, which every agent is; he judges them with human eyes, and the coordinator helps him write them in the most concise and precise way. Agent shorthand stays where it serves; an internal reference that the record established is explained, since without FACTS, POSITIONS and INDEX in context neither he nor an agent can follow it. The skill corrects the assumptions an agent brings from pre-training, so that it forms a correct model of Fortress as a language before it works on the repository that implements it: a "Fortress as a language" section stands in the opening of `fortress-repo`'s `SKILL.md`, which every agent reads, since nearly every agent writes a test program (`explorations/reviews/fortress-pretraining-quiz.md`, `explorations/reviews/skills-distillation-audit.md` section D). Where a practice is in question, an archaeology worker checks what the batch workers actually did, as the search for their use of the `fortress` commands did, so that where a better way was found and agents still work around the issue, the skill states the better way. The register is about halfway toward ASD-STE100, Simplified Technical English: one instruction per sentence in the imperative, active voice, short sentences, the condition before the instruction, one word for one thing, no telegraphic or essayist shorthand, and no sentence that tells the reader neither what to do nor a fact needed to do it; without bloating the skills, whose token count matters, a rewrite never longer than what it replaces. Skill text follows ASD-STE100's split: a description of the concepts first, then short procedures, stated positively. He suspects the essayist register came into the skills from FACTS and POSITIONS, written in it by earlier models. Building happens in one area at a time (the interpreter, the compiled path, the library, the specification), which is why the parts are split by area; the skill says so in those terms. `SKILL.md` holds the concepts and the trigger to switch modes; the exploring procedure is a part loaded when the trigger fires. Knowledge that daily work needs stands in the part an agent loads when it needs it; a note stays named in a skill only with a trigger, when to open it, and a narrow way to read it. The record (FACTS, POSITIONS, the ledger, INDEX and the notes) is the project's memory, kept in the repository because a session can move to another container; `fortress-repo` defines it near its top, with its files, and the `coordinator` skill picks each worker's slice of it, so that the dependency runs one way and a worker never needs the `coordinator` skill. How a Claude Code session behaves (long commands, waits, interrupts, the permission check) belongs in `fortress-repo`, which every worker has loaded, not in a skill of its own; what only the coordinator needs of it goes to `coordinator`. The checker count and the distance are the ladder's metrics: the workflow's prompts say how to run them and the `coordinator` skill names them; `fortress-repo` holds nothing of them. `fortress-repo` carries no batch structure, on purpose: which roles exist, who builds, checks, runs the gate, commits and pushes to `main` are set by whoever writes the workflow or the brief, so that agents designing a workflow from the skill are not steered toward ours; the skill says how to do each thing, and the brief says which an agent does. The role split may later be written as behaviours a batch's instructions point to, in a skill of its own. An agent acts on its brief alone: the skill names no other authority over what it commits or pushes, and no rule an agent cannot act on (it cannot ask him). A worker works with three things, its brief, the record and the original tree, in one of two modes: building, where it stands on the record and carries out its mission, and exploring, where it has met something the record does not settle (a gap, two of the team's sources in conflict, new evidence against a decision), gathers evidence in the record first and then in the original tree, adds what it found to the record and returns to building. Where the team's sources disagree, the later one wins, the specification included. A worker never settles such a conflict itself, and never revises the specification to a later source on its own reading: the conflict is surfaced, and the curator rules after research by the nine steps; a decision on record is carried out, and questioned only on evidence the decision did not use. A conflict between the text and an implementation that no decision settles gets a ledger row and a test of the current behaviour; an `XXX` test belongs to repair work, where the current behaviour is tolerated for a time on purpose and the test is promoted once the fix is in. What a worker builds on while its question waits for him is left unsaid: it is case by case, and a reversible choice takes the path of least resistance without a rule. An agent takes the tree it starts from as green, starts with its own failing test, reuses only what its brief cites or its own work ran, and runs more only where its edit reaches; test first applies to an edit of source code. A point a brief wants reviewed is a "point to report", never a "stop", which reads as a condition that stops the worker. A skill's parts are written by the seven writing principles (`explorations/reviews/skills-writing-principles.md`), which he has read and judged safe to apply to every rewrite: the writer applies them to a whole part at once, and he then reads it, not comment by comment. The earlier lenses note and the review-style profile are not a writer's brief. Before he reads a rewritten part, a cold reader given only the skill reads it as the workers who use it would, flags each passage that would send it wrong or make it search, with its confidence, the undefined terms and the gotchas; the coordinator reviews the findings, the writer fixes them with his comments so far, and only then does he read it. A comment on the review page marks a problem area, not a sentence to patch: the fix is made at the level of its paragraph or section, with the whole skill in mind. The skills are rewritten in place, git holding the earlier text. One writer does the writing, briefed by the coordinator, every comment fix on the page among its tasks, made from the coordinator's brief of his comment instead of the coordinator interpreting his words: it edits the skill files and commits them, and the coordinator keeps the review page in step, so that the writer's context holds only the writing. The writer is a headless Claude session (`explorations/coordinator/tools/skill-writer.sh`, its standing brief `explorations/coordinator/skill-writer-brief.md`), since such a session keeps its prompt cache for an hour, as the coordinating session does, while an Agent-tool subagent keeps it for five minutes and so writes its whole context again when the curator's next comment comes later than that; the cache's one-hour writes cost more and are worth it. It is resumed for each writing task, and started fresh with only its standing brief when its history no longer serves, as after a large pass or the coordinator's compaction. Its standing order: ASD-STE100 at least halfway, terms defined first and then used, concise procedures, positive phrasing; and it does not take the register of what it reads, since FACTS, POSITIONS and many notes were written by earlier models in agent shorthand that the curator cannot follow cold, and the coordinator, which carries them in context, keeps drifting into it. A writer's minor decisions not taken are settled by the coordinator from the record, as his executive assistant, and not put to him; he is asked only where a rule would change its meaning or a new rule would enter. A position's bold title here is the record's handle, not his words, and is never quoted to him as his. A glossary of terms, in a skill or on a page, is written as a dictionary is: each term and the noun phrase that opens its definition with no article ("revival", not "the revival").

Its goals: one home for how the coordinator works, the protocol retired and its rules that bind workers moved to `fortress-repo`; every kind of record with one home and a stated purpose, the informal homes (the handover's first section, the held list, PLAN's old rule sections, the boot note's standing lines) retired or folded in, and the skill saying plainly what goes where; a lighter boot that keeps his awareness (the coordinator knows the project without him, never re-asks a decision, never sends a worker to measure again what is on record), the open question being FACTS, POSITIONS and INDEX (their form, a grouping by topic, what they repeat of each other, what is always loaded and what is looked up), settled on evidence and a trial boot, not assumed; FACTS, POSITIONS and INDEX keep serving workers' briefs; no role lost (executive assistant, batch coordinator, roadmap architect, briefer of workers, analyst); the `fortress-repo` writing standard. The skill is tiered: a core always loaded, and sections loaded when the coordinator switches into a mode of work that is not needed all the time (running and reviewing a batch, process engineering, a post-mortem, among others), as the workers' skill separates building from exploring; the coordinator's modes are identified and described first. The batch workflow is in scope, since it is improved whenever it shows a problem. `cloud-container` follows by the `fortress-repo` method. The skill guides lightly, by a few general principles, among them how to talk to him in each situation, and the record is organized as the model organizes knowledge by itself: "Organization of the knowledge base should reflect the model's second nature, never fight it", which he names the rewrite's highest risk. So the natural baseline of the current models, Fable 5.1 and Opus 5.5, is established before the design, and the design starts from what this project's environment adds to it: his review on a phone, dictation by speech to text, holding the open items of a long dictated message (preventing the wall-of-text spiral and recovering from it), and keeping a large body of project knowledge in order. The map also gives where each record home came from and which model made it. The map of the shape the rewrite starts from is `coordinator/process-engineering/coordinator-shape.md`. The skill is written from scratch by an Opus writer, from the sort of the rules against the baseline (what is kept, and the facts stated instead of rules), the map's problems to design against, and the writing principles; the writer does not read the existing skill, which is not refactored. A check against the sort's list after the draft shows whether anything kept was lost, and then the cold read follows as usual. A rule is kept when the failure it guards against is on record; the interview's self-report decides no drop, since the two disagree exactly on the rules that matter (the clock, closed decisions), and the interview showed only the record's default shape, a short current-state file with decisions and history apart, which is kept without instruction. - P-148 **The checker's speed comes right after its correctness.** A rung that changes the checker measures how the change affects the time that the checker needs. First the rung makes the change correct. Then it does a second pass that makes the checker faster. This pass must not change the checker's results: the checker accepts and refuses the same programs as before, with the same errors at the same places. The rung's skeptic checks the time and the results. This work is part of the rung, not a separate step after the landing. ### P-40

2026-10-20 05:43Z · rewritten · from `explorations/perf-probes/array-sites/REPORT.md`, "Step 2: E3 re-based (`e3-rebased.patch`)" · by curator: his answer to ask 3 in chat, 05:42 UTC on 2026-10-20: option 1, the diff; 'The patch made sense to me.'

- P-40 **Sizes are checked, and a size left unknown is an error only where it is used.** The checker checks `nat` and `int` static parameters and refuses a size beyond `NN32` or `ZZ32` (F-140). Storage refuses a JVM array length over 2^31-1. A size that inference leaves unknown is an error only when it reaches a type, a name or a value. How the checker represents it is not his choice. A size that a call cannot fix refuses the call, also inside an overload set, instead of dropping the arm (row 400, its compiled half open). Every static argument but an operator counts in the exclusion rule; booleans join once the checker has boolean parameters (row 406). A size used as a value converts to `ZZ32` as a numeral does (row 485). The api's 25 unused sizes (F-35) go only by a diff shown to him with the switch-over's design.

### P-153

2026-10-20 12:51Z · rewritten · from `explorations/compile-ladder/climb-batch-20/RECORD.md`, "FACTS and POSITIONS left for the coordinator" · commit `5ad4a9957` · by climb batch 20's routing: built by rung I, with three demos where it named two; "Still to build" narrowed to the compiled half

- P-153 **An integer raised to a negative power raises an error; a fraction comes from a float or rational base (contract E, item 58).** Each integer type's `^` answers its own type at every exponent: a negative exponent raises `IntegerOverflow` where the result is no integer, a base of 0 raises `DivisionByZero`, and the bases 1 and -1 give their exact power. A program that wants a fraction writes a float or rational base, as `2.0^(-1)`. Why: the team's later code types an integer power by its integer at a signed exponent (P-26); overloads choose by type, and no type carries an exponent's or a numeral's sign, so the split is on the base, with the sign tested at run time as the library tests every sign-refined subset (P-80). Still to build: the integer powers so typed, with row 747's typed `fail` in `strToInt`, and the team tests `realArith`, `compoundArray` and `simpleExp` and two demos written with a float base (PLAN item 58).

A hardware behaviour that bytecode can express gets its own explicit spelling, as the specification's wrapping and saturating operators do. Where the JVM prevents a correct implementation, the project gives way for now. The peers to compare are Julia, X10, Swift and Rust.

The idea of a split is his: 2026-10-19T23:55:49.331Z, answering the coordinator's 23:33:57.594Z first form of ask 2 (E, Z, R and X): a combination of options, and whether overloads can split the negative exponents from the positive; and 2026-10-20T00:01:06.669Z, answering the coordinator's 2026-10-19T23:57:18.359Z: launch Fable with that question.

- P-153 **An integer raised to a negative power raises an error; a fraction comes from a float or rational base (contract E, item 58).** Each integer type's `^` answers its own type at every exponent: a negative exponent raises `IntegerOverflow` where the result is no integer, a base of 0 raises `DivisionByZero`, and the bases 1 and -1 give their exact power. A program that wants a fraction writes a float or rational base, as `2.0^(-1)`. Why: the team's later code types an integer power by its integer at a signed exponent (P-26); overloads choose by type, and no type carries an exponent's or a numeral's sign, so the split is on the base, with the sign tested at run time as the library tests every sign-refined subset (P-80). Built in the one library and under walk. Still to build: the compiled path's own powers by the same contract, which answer 0 at a negative exponent today, when phase 4's natives bind the one library's `^` (rows 441 and 523; PLAN, phase 4, the natives).

### P-153

2026-10-22 08:30Z · rewritten · by the clarity pass: rewritten for a reader without the conversation, meaning unchanged from the audit's proposal (positions-audit/clarity/evidence/pilot/proposals.md, "P-153"); his "Yes." of 2026-10-20T00:44:34.730Z to option 1 of 00:19:08.321Z, and the split that he recalled at 05:56:19.215Z; the label "contract E" struck; built by rung I, 5ad4a9957

- P-153 **The integer power splits on the base: an integer base gives an integer or raises an error, and a float or rational base gives the fraction (contract E, item 58).** Each integer type's `^` answers its own type at every exponent: a negative exponent raises `IntegerOverflow` where the result is no integer, a base of 0 raises `DivisionByZero`, and the bases 1 and -1 give their exact power. A program that wants a fraction writes a float or rational base, as `2.0^(-1)`. Why: the team's later code types an integer power by its integer at a signed exponent (P-26); overloads choose by type, and no type carries an exponent's or a numeral's sign, so the split is on the base, with the sign tested at run time as the library tests every sign-refined subset (P-80). Still to build: the compiled path's powers by the same contract (rows 441 and 523; PLAN, phase 4, the natives).

### P-26

The type group then made the language run, for performance and with specialization a must, and learned what is feasible. So their later word wins: the exclusion rule, specialization kept, the compiler prelude's tower flattened in 2009 and 2010 (P-43), Naden's 2012 write-up, the POPL 2019 paper. - P-148 **The checker's speed comes right after its correctness.** A rung that adds to the checker first makes the change correct, then, in the same rung, makes the new code fast without changing which programs the checker accepts or refuses, so that no optimization is left for after the landing. The aim is a checker that stays usable as it grows: some slowdown is fine, but new checks have made it orders of magnitude slower, enough to hold a landing. The timing is a smoke test: one run, to catch a slowdown of several times; a small difference is noise. Built in the `fortress-repo` skill (`compiler.md`, "The checker's time").

- P-125 **The suites run Fortress in parallel, pinned at four threads in `build.xml`.** Parallelism is native to Fortress, so a one-thread suite has a blind spot, and sparing a four-CPU machine is no reason for one. A test that fails or varies under parallelism is written wrong, such as a write that needs `atomic`, or it exposes a defect: either is fixed, never hidden by a pin. The pin makes every run the same, whatever the calling shell sets. Built.

### P-17

- P-17 **Unboxed doubles and arrays of unboxed doubles are required: without them the revival misses its point.** Why: a language meant for high performance cannot box its numbers. In Java models of microGPT's three array kernels, the dot product, the matrix product and the row lift, boxing is the main cost (F-11 holds the measurement). How arrays store doubles, as `double[]`, is P-84's decision. ### P-40

2026-10-22 12:37Z · rewritten · by the scrape: status and planning references deleted, at the curator's word of 2026-10-22

- P-40 **Sizes are checked, and a size that inference leaves unknown is an error only where it is used.** A size is a `nat` or `int` static parameter, such as the length of an array in its type. The compiled checker checks sizes, and refuses a size beyond `NN32` or `ZZ32` (F-140, the bound on both paths). Wherever the array design creates a JVM array, it checks the length there, at run time, and refuses one over 2^31-1. A size that inference leaves unknown is an error only when it reaches a type, a name or a value. So a declaration may declare a size that none of its types uses, and pass. A call that cannot fix a size of the declaration that it would choose is refused, also inside an overload set, instead of passing over that declaration. In the rule that no type extends two different instantiations of one generic trait, two instantiations differ when any static argument but an operator differs, so `Vec[\3\]` and `Vec[\4\]` are two; boolean arguments count too once the checker has boolean parameters. A size used as a number, as in the range `0#n`, converts to `ZZ32` where one is needed, as a numeral does. The 17 vector and matrix operators of the `FortressLibrary` api lose their unused sizes by `explorations/perf-probes/array-sites/e3-rebased.patch`, which finishes the team's own fix of 2008: that fix removed them from the component only. Built, except three parts. The JVM length check waits for the array design (PLAN, phase 5); under walk such an array dies with a raw Java exception (gap-ledger row 527). The checker still passes over a declaration whose unfixed size occurs in its own parameter type (row 400). The checker compares no boolean arguments (row 406).

### P-45

2026-10-22 12:37Z · rewritten · by the scrape: status and planning references deleted, at the curator's word of 2026-10-22

- P-45 **Overloaded declarations may differ in their static parameters.** The specification drops its sentence that forbids this, and keeps it as superseded text. It states instead the overloading model of the type group's 2011 paper (`Papers/Types/`), which the compiled checker already used. The checker keeps the paper's three rules: No Duplicates, Meet and Return Type. It checks the Return Type Rule over every instance of a generic declaration that the argument types allow, not over one guessed instance. One rule of the old sentence survives: if one generic declaration is more specific than another, their static parameters must line up position by position, as Java's rule for overriding a generic method requires. Walk chooses among overloads by the parameter types that each declaration declares, not by the order in which they are written. At load, walk judges each pair of two generic declarations by the checker's rules on their declared parameter types. It does not refuse a pair only because none of their parameter types exclude, as when one declaration is more specific than the other. Walk and the checker must accept the same pairs. The library repairs, by declarations of its own, the overload sets that the checker refused, and no line of microGPT's two programs changes. Still to build, where walk or the checker still judges a pair against these rules: walk compares two functional methods of a generic trait or object on the instance that a call makes (F-26); both paths refuse the paper's own valid example (gap-ledger row 539); walk refuses two nested generic functional methods of a plain trait, and fails at a call on them in a generic trait (rows 779 and 780); and walk and the checker disagree on four kinds of pair (rows 759 and 786 to 788). So beside `op(a: Any, b: Any)`, `op[\T extends Number\](a: T, b: T)` applies to a call with a `ZZ32` and a `ZZ64`, at `T = Number`. It is the more specific, so it runs, at `ZZ64`. Why: the curator wants fast generic code that a JVM can specialise. His own broader rule is not taken: to apply any conversion that reaches a specific declaration, and to fall back to a plain one only when none does. Walk and the compiled checker both infer static arguments with coercion, by the same promotion. The compiled path inserts the conversions statically. Walk, which has no static types, promotes from the arguments' run-time types: a choice taken without the curator's word, which waits for his review (review-queue item 85). ### P-48

As built, a generic declaration of `f` is first instantiated from the other arguments. Built under walk, and on the compiled path for `ZZ32` and `ZZ64` with a count of the shifted value's own type. - P-153 **The integer power splits on the base: an integer base gives an integer or raises an error, and a float or rational base gives the fraction.** Each integer type's `^` returns its own type at every exponent. At a negative exponent, the bases 1 and -1 give their exact power, a base of 0 raises `DivisionByZero`, and any other base raises `IntegerOverflow`. A program that wants a fraction writes a float or rational base, as in `2.0^(-1)`. Why: the team's later code gives an integer power the type of its integer base at a signed exponent (P-26, the team's later work outweighs the specification's early text). Overloads choose by type, and no type tells a negative exponent from a non-negative one, since a signed integer or a numeral can be either. So the split is on the base, and the sign of the exponent is tested at run time, as the library tests a value's sign everywhere (P-80, run-time sign tests in place of sign-refined types). Built in the interpreter's library and under walk, at an exponent of any integer type (F-263 states it as built). Still to build: the same rule on the compiled path, whose own powers give 0 at a negative exponent and a raw Java exception at a base of 0 (gap-ledger row 441), and overflow silently or with a raw Java exception (row 523); PLAN, phase 4, the natives, binds the library's `^` to compiled helpers.

### P-60

2026-10-22 12:37Z · rewritten · by the scrape: status and planning references deleted, at the curator's word of 2026-10-22

- P-60 **The specification reads a `comprises` clause at the level of values.** `trait S comprises { U, V }` says that every value of `S` is a value of `U` or of `V`. So another trait may stand between `S` and its listed types. The specification defines "covers" once: a type covers another when every value of the second is a value of the first. The Meet Rule gains a case for closed traits. Where the rule asks for a declaration at the meet of two overloads' parameter types, several declarations below both serve, if together they cover the meet. So a call whose argument's static type stands between closed traits and their listed types may have several most specific declarations. The call's type is the intersection of their return types, and the declaration that runs is unique only at run time. Why: the proof addendum (`reviews/comprises-type-level-proof-addendum.md`) shows this sound for ground declarations, at calls that need no coercion. A ground declaration's types hold no type variable, and `G[\ZZ32\]` is ground. The addendum also asks that the checker use coverage only in its overloading rules, to accept an overload set and to justify a call's tie among several most specific declarations, and not in its general subtype test. A declaration with static parameters, whose instance dispatch must choose at run time, is outside the proof: one instance would have to meet every one of the return types at once. The typing by the intersection is not extended to it, and the specification states that boundary. Built: F-175 (the specification), F-76 (the compiled checker) and F-27 (walk's load check).

In its method bodies, `self` has the type `C[\X\] & X`. The method signatures keep the plain trait type, because the proofs of the POPL 2019 paper on multiple dispatch (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu) assume that no declared type holds a type parameter inside an intersection. The checker refuses a type that extends such a trait at another type, since an extender must be a subtype of the static argument that it gives: `object Bad extends Integral[\ZZ32\]` is refused, because `Bad` is not a `ZZ32`. Scala has the same rule for self types. The library's text gains no self type and no `comprises` clause, and walk is unchanged. The rule accepts every self-typed method body of the library except `Integral`'s `numerator`, which each integer type declares instead. Why: the checker reads the library as the team wrote it, as P-44 does for the closure of `AnyIntegral`, by one rule that peer languages share. Built (F-225 states the rule as built).

### P-40

2026-10-22 22:14Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/consolidation-2026-10-22/evidence/positions-followup/proposals.md`, "P-40" · by the POSITIONS follow-up writer (Opus): the cold read found the patch's state unclear and the array design undefined; the patch is in the tree (e487cec2d; F-35), said in the present; the array design defined by P-85; applied at the curator's word of 22:12 UTC on 2026-10-22, \"apply all the improvements drafted\", without his review of each change

- P-40 **Sizes are checked, and a size that inference leaves unknown is an error only where it is used.** A size is a `nat` or `int` static parameter, such as the length of an array in its type. The compiled checker checks sizes, and refuses a size beyond `NN32` or `ZZ32` (F-140, the bound on both paths). Wherever the array design creates a JVM array, it checks the length there, at run time, and refuses one over 2^31-1. A size that inference leaves unknown is an error only when it reaches a type, a name or a value. So a declaration may declare a size that none of its types uses, and pass. A call that cannot fix a size of the declaration that it would choose is refused, also inside an overload set, instead of passing over that declaration. In the rule that no type extends two different instantiations of one generic trait, two instantiations differ when any static argument but an operator differs, so `Vec[\3\]` and `Vec[\4\]` are two; boolean arguments count too once the checker has boolean parameters. A size used as a number, as in the range `0#n`, converts to `ZZ32` where one is needed, as a numeral does. The 17 vector and matrix operators of the `FortressLibrary` api lose their unused sizes by `explorations/perf-probes/array-sites/e3-rebased.patch`, which finishes the team's own fix of 2008: that fix removed them from the component only. The JVM length check waits for the array design; under walk such an array dies with a raw Java exception (row 527). The checker still passes over a declaration whose unfixed size occurs in its own parameter type (row 400). The checker compares no boolean arguments (row 406).

### P-45

2026-10-22 22:14Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/consolidation-2026-10-22/evidence/positions-followup/proposals.md`, "P-45" · by the POSITIONS follow-up writer (Opus): the cold read found the positional rule's form and enforcement unclear, and nested read as declared inside; the checker tests the rule only where the two declarations have as many static parameters of their own (OverloadingOracle.scala:164), a scope that F-75 calls the rung's and review-queue item 60 holds; rows 779 and 780 said by their claims; applied at the curator's word of 22:12 UTC on 2026-10-22, \"apply all the improvements drafted\", without his review of each change

- P-45 **Overloaded declarations may differ in their static parameters.** The specification drops its sentence that forbids this, and keeps it as superseded text. It states instead the overloading model of the type group's 2011 paper (`Papers/Types/`), which the compiled checker already used. The checker keeps the paper's three rules: No Duplicates, Meet and Return Type. It checks the Return Type Rule over every instance of a generic declaration that the argument types allow, not over one guessed instance. One rule of the old sentence survives: if one generic declaration is more specific than another, their static parameters must line up position by position, as Java's rule for overriding a generic method requires. Walk chooses among overloads by the parameter types that each declaration declares, not by the order in which they are written. At load, walk judges each pair of two generic declarations by the checker's rules on their declared parameter types. It does not refuse a pair only because none of their parameter types exclude, as when one declaration is more specific than the other. Walk and the checker must accept the same pairs. The library repairs, by declarations of its own, the overload sets that the checker refused, and no line of microGPT's two programs changes. Where walk or the checker still judges a pair against these rules: walk compares two functional methods of a generic trait or object on the instance that a call makes (F-26); both paths refuse the paper's own valid example (row 539); walk refuses two nested generic functional methods of a plain trait, and fails at a call on them in a generic trait (rows 779 and 780); and walk and the checker disagree on four kinds of pair (rows 759 and 786 to 788).

- P-46 **A conversion only makes a call possible: it never changes which declaration runs when one already fits.** The declaration that runs is chosen on the parameter types that each overload declares: first those that apply, then the most specific of them (P-45). A generic declaration applies if some type within its bounds takes the arguments. Only then is the chosen declaration instantiated by promotion: at the narrowest type into which its arguments coerce (P-74). So beside `op(a: Any, b: Any)`, `op[\T extends Number\](a: T, b: T)` applies to a call with a `ZZ32` and a `ZZ64`, at `T = Number`. It is the more specific, so it runs, at `ZZ64`. Why: the curator wants fast generic code that a JVM can specialise. His own broader rule is not taken: to apply any conversion that reaches a specific declaration, and to fall back to a plain one only when none does. Walk and the compiled checker both infer static arguments with coercion, by the same promotion. The compiled path inserts the conversions statically. Walk, which has no static types, promotes from the arguments' run-time types: a choice taken without the curator's word. P-49 gives the order in which the checker tries a call. Row 537: the compiled dispatch between two generic declarations of one name with different bounds.

### P-60

2026-10-22 22:14Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/consolidation-2026-10-22/evidence/positions-followup/proposals.md`, "P-60" · by the POSITIONS follow-up writer (Opus): the cold read found the addendum's ask inside the Why, the coercion case open and no row; the ask stated as part of his yes of 2026-09-29T11:25:37.094Z (Astra's points), the addendum's lines 186-192; the coercion boundary from the addendum's lines 14-25 and Specification/advanced/overloading.tex:716-719, the tree F-76's; rows 543 and 551 named; applied at the curator's word of 22:12 UTC on 2026-10-22, \"apply all the improvements drafted\", without his review of each change

- P-60 **The specification reads a `comprises` clause at the level of values.** `trait S comprises { U, V }` says that every value of `S` is a value of `U` or of `V`. So another trait may stand between `S` and its listed types. The specification defines "covers" once: a type covers another when every value of the second is a value of the first. The Meet Rule gains a case for closed traits. Where the rule asks for a declaration at the meet of two overloads' parameter types, several declarations below both serve, if together they cover the meet. So a call whose argument's static type stands between closed traits and their listed types may have several most specific declarations. The call's type is the intersection of their return types, and the declaration that runs is unique only at run time. Why: the proof addendum (`reviews/comprises-type-level-proof-addendum.md`) shows this sound for ground declarations, at calls that need no coercion. A ground declaration's types hold no type variable, and `G[\ZZ32\]` is ground. The addendum also asks that the checker use coverage only in its overloading rules, to accept an overload set and to justify a call's tie among several most specific declarations, and not in its general subtype test. A declaration with static parameters, whose instance dispatch must choose at run time, is outside the proof: one instance would have to meet every one of the return types at once. The typing by the intersection is not extended to it, and the specification states that boundary.

### P-88

2026-10-22 22:14Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/consolidation-2026-10-22/evidence/positions-followup/proposals.md`, "P-88" · by the POSITIONS follow-up writer (Opus): the cold read found the read-only matrix trait without a row; the drafted row named (row 798), his page comment of 2026-10-21 23:29 UTC keeping the sentence; applied at the curator's word of 22:12 UTC on 2026-10-22, \"apply all the improvements drafted\", without his review of each change

- P-88 **The model's vocabulary leaves `+`, `-` and `MAX` between an array and a scalar to the library's scalar-extension block, and its diagonal keeps its own product.** The vocabulary, the array operations that microGPT is written in, declares none of these six operators, an array with a number on either side; the library's scalar-extension block, in `Library/FortressLibrary.fss`, serves them. `Diag`, the vocabulary's diagonal matrix, extends `Matrix` and overrides `mul`, the method that the library's matrix product calls, so `diag(v) m` scales the rows of `m` without forming the matrix. This is what the type system promises: a diagonal is a kind of matrix, with a faster product of its own. Row 299 records what the general product costs on a diagonal: 13 to 54 times as much as scaling the rows. Since the diagonal is read-only, a read-only matrix trait, `ReadableArray2`, which `Array2` would extend as `Array1` extends `ReadableArray1`, is also wanted: it is a library change, not a definition in the vocabulary.

### P-151

2026-10-22 22:14Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/consolidation-2026-10-22/evidence/positions-followup/proposals.md`, "P-151" · by the POSITIONS follow-up writer (Opus): the cold read found the move of addLeft and addRight without a row and the distribute sentence against F-242; the batch's departure goes, F-243 states it, review-queue item 188 holds it; the drafted row named (row 799); the distribute sentence keeps only his option 3 of 2026-10-16 (measure the team's commented fix for the last two errors) and F-242's finding that the team's text only moves them; the form that clears them stays in F-242 alone, since his HOLD of 2026-10-16T20:09:30.180Z left that default unanswered; applied at the curator's word of 22:12 UTC on 2026-10-22, \"apply all the improvements drafted\", without his review of each change

- P-151 **Only the `where`-clause forms that the team wrote down are built: conditional extension, a function's or a method's condition, and `AnyList`'s `comprises` clause.** A `where` clause states conditions on static parameters, and may introduce static variables of its own, as `where [\E\]` does. A trait or object that extends a type under a `where` condition is that type only where the condition holds, and its members are checked with the condition holding (conditional extension). A `where` condition on a function's or a method's own static parameters bounds them. The team's commented-out clause `AnyList comprises List[\E\] where [\E\]` becomes live, which clears the type error of `AnyList`'s join `||`; its other operations, `addLeft` and `addRight`, move into `List`. `SUM`'s fusion with `MAX` and `MIN`, the library's fast path for the maximum or the minimum of sums, stays. A method's `where` condition clears four of the fusion's six type errors, and the team's commented-out generic `distribute`, after a measurement, the other two (F-242: the errors and how each is cleared). Why: a feature with little support in the team's work bends the language beyond what the curator can review. He has such a feature built where the team left enough design intent for it, or where it is on the critical path to running fast compiled. Climb batch 17 kept `addLeft` and `addRight` in `AnyList` instead, respelled under the live clause, against the option he chose. Row 773: the members scoped to the condition, since a member checked under it stays callable where the condition fails. Row 774: walk's half of conditional extension. Row 701: the code generator's half.

### P-155

2026-10-23 07:10Z · rewritten · by coordinator: the two designs P-155 brought to the curator are decided, P-156 and P-157

- P-155 **The compiled checker checks the overloads of symbolic operators before the switch-over.** Today its overloading check reads an operator only if its name is a word of capitals, such as `MAX`, so `+`, `^`, `||`, the subscripts and the other symbolic operators pass unchecked. The check must read them by the rules that it applies to any function, so that the distance to the switch-over reaches zero with them checked. Why: the skip is a gap that the team knew, not a rule: the specification's appendix of proposed features calls the library's two subscript operators "not really a valid overloading" (`Specification/appendices/future.tex:186-207`, its item on array indexing). A zero with them unchecked would hide known errors. Each design that the check needs is brought to the curator before its rung, with the probe's measurement (`perf-probes/symbolic-operators-2/REPORT.md`, which counts the new errors and tries designs for the two cases that need one: a bound on the subscripts' index type that keeps ranges out; and, for the arrays' `+` of a vector and a number beside `+` of two vectors, vectors that stop being additive groups, or the scalar `+` and `-` dropped). Row 584: the check and its rungs.

### P-156

2026-10-23 12:38Z · rewritten · from `explorations/coordinator/CLIMB-BATCH-21.md`, "Question 2, consequential: whether `BoundedRange`'s narrowed `atMost` goes": his answer to batch 21's question 2 at 12:36:55 UTC on 2026-10-23: at most agreed with recommendation option 2

- P-156 **A collection's index type extends `Index`, a trait that the ranges exclude, and a tuple of indices is an `Index`.** Every index parameter of the library's generic indexed types is bounded `I extends Index`; `ZZ32` extends `Index`, and `Range` excludes it. So a subscript that takes one index and a subscript that takes a range never apply to the same value, and the pair is a valid overloading at every instance. A tuple type whose elements are all `Index` types is itself an `Index`, on both paths and in the specification: a typing rule that changes no value, so `m[i, j]` passes its tuple `(i, j)` as before. Why: it is the team's own fix for this pair (`Specification/appendices/future.tex:186-207`), its "magic" read as subtyping. A `where` constraint `I excludes Range[\I\]` was weighed and not taken: the team left such a constraint as an open question, and the code generator refuses a `where` clause in a declaration's header. Row 783: the subscript pair.

- P-156 **A collection's index type extends `Index`, a trait that the ranges exclude, and a tuple of indices is an `Index`.** Every index parameter of the library's generic indexed types is bounded `I extends Index`; `ZZ32` extends `Index`, and `Range` excludes it. So a subscript that takes one index and a subscript that takes a range never apply to the same value, and the pair is a valid overloading at every instance. A tuple type whose elements are all `Index` types is itself an `Index`, on both paths and in the specification: a typing rule that changes no value, so `m[i, j]` passes its tuple `(i, j)` as before. Why: it is the team's own fix for this pair (`Specification/appendices/future.tex:186-207`), its "magic" read as subtyping. A `where` constraint `I excludes Range[\I\]` was weighed and not taken: the team left such a constraint as an open question, and the code generator refuses a `where` clause in a declaration's header. `BoundedRange`, which cannot take the bound, since the open range `(:)` stays a range over `Any` (P-56), keeps its narrowed `atMost(n: I): FullRange[\I\]` under the method condition `where { I extends Index }`, the library's form for a member that applies at some instances only; after the switch-over the code generator's refusal of a method with a `where` clause stops it too, as it stops `SumReduction`'s two `distribute`s (row 701). ### P-157

2026-10-23 13:05Z · rewritten · from `explorations/perf-probes/self-type-exclusion/REPORT.md`, "In short": his answer to batch 21's vectors ask at 13:03:22 UTC on 2026-10-23, option 1, reversing P-157 as decided at 07:09 UTC: Yes, of course, option one. That was my original stance, so you talked me out of it this morning and the reason prevailed. On the type-decisions judgement (reviews/type-decisions-review/judgement.md, V1) and probe A1.

- P-157 **`Vector` and `Matrix` are not additive groups for now, so the arrays' scalar `+` and `-` stand beside the vectors' own.** They keep their own `+`, `-` and unary `-`, but no longer extend `AdditiveGroup`, and arrays exclude every additive group, as `Array3` already does. So generic code over `AdditiveGroup`, such as `SUM` over vectors or `zero`, no longer takes them. Why: the scalar block that microGPT uses (P-88) and the team's additive-group vectors cannot both pass the overload check while it reads a self-typed trait's `self` in a signature as the plain trait type (P-146); the library's consistency gives way, since it must not hold up microGPT. Vectors may become additive groups again once the checker reads `self` as the implementing type in the overload check.

### P-146

2026-10-23 13:05Z · rewritten · from `explorations/perf-probes/self-type-exclusion/REPORT.md`, "In short": P-157 as he decided it at 13:03:22 UTC on 2026-10-23: the exclusion test alone reads self as the implementing type

- P-146 **In a self-typed trait, the compiled checker types `self` in the method bodies as also being the implementing type.** A self-typed trait is a trait `C[\X\]` whose parameter `X` extends `C[\X\]`, such as `Integral[\I extends Integral[\I\]\]`. In its method bodies, `self` has the type `C[\X\] & X`. The method signatures keep the plain trait type, because the proofs of the POPL 2019 paper on multiple dispatch (Park, Hong, Steele and Ryu) assume that no declared type holds a type parameter inside an intersection. The checker refuses a type that extends such a trait at another type, since an extender must be a subtype of the static argument that it gives: `object Bad extends Integral[\ZZ32\]` is refused, because `Bad` is not a `ZZ32`. Scala has the same rule for self types. The library's text gains no self type and no `comprises` clause, and walk is unchanged. The rule accepts every self-typed method body of the library except `Integral`'s `numerator`, which each integer type declares instead. Why: the checker reads the library as the team wrote it, as P-44 does for the closure of `AnyIntegral`, by one rule that peer languages share. F-225 states the rule as built.
